And in the silence that followed — in the space between the departure and the journey, in the gap between the leaving and the returning, in the charged stillness of a ship riding the wind with a butterfly at the helm of a man's shoulder — the sound of the music played on.


---

## Chapter 32: The Moon Remembers

The epilogue of the Fall was the resonance of a symphony that had finally learned how to play its own mistakes.

Lukas Wolfheart stood at the edge of the "Great Geyser" in Heimfeld — a massive, crystalline formation that erupted from the dwarven city's central plaza in a column of silver-violet light that stretched from the cobblestones to the Aegis-Shield overhead. The geyser was not a natural phenomenon. It was a Rift — a dimensional wound left behind by the Maw's collapse, a place where the barrier between the physical world and the void-space of the system's processing architecture had been worn thin by the frequency of the final battle, and where the old Discord still leaked through in a slow, wounded pulse of violet-black light. The dwarven stonemasons had built their city around it, centuries before anyone understood what it was; they had worshipped its light, feared its dark, and finally — under Lukas's new order — learned to tend it the way a healer tends a patient that will not close.

The plaza around the geyser was a particular, layered record of that tending. White-wood scaffolds leaned against the crystalline flanks, their beams scarred by the particular, chemical burn of repeated resonance-work. Buckets of limestone-dust and binding-sap stood in a tidy row where a Heimfeld mender had left them, each one labeled in the squared hand of a people who measured even their mercy. And at the geyser's base, where the system's processing had long ago etched its clinical runes into the stone, the grey marble still held — a smooth patch of finalized silence in the middle of a city that had chosen to be loud.

They were no longer soldiers. They were the "Menders."

The Menders were a new breed of hero — hybrids and humans who had been trained in the art of dimensional repair, who had learned to read the frequency of the Rifts and coax them closed with the silver-light of the Ascension and the violet fire of the Aegis. They traveled the Seven Realms in small, swift ships, their hands mending the cracks in the world's fabric, their voices singing the songs that the Rifts needed to hear in order to heal. They did not fight. They listened. They did not conquer. They tended. And Lukas — the Wolfheart who had once held the Aegis together by the exhausting force of his refusing to dissolve — had become, in the months since the Open Road had opened before him, their first and strangest pupil: a weapon who had learned to be a tool.

He raised his spear — the Spire, that ancient weapon of the Ascension, its blade still whined with the resonance of every battle it had fought, its Rift-Iron shaft warm in his grip with the familiar weight of an old friend. He poured the noise of his history into the finalized marble that surrounded the geyser's base — not the blazing, defiant pillar of the old Ascension but the quiet, focused needle of the new, the surgeon's light that could mend without breaking. His silver light flowed through the spear's shaft and into the stone, warming it, mending it, coaxing it back from the clinical grey of finalization to the warm, living color of the world that had been.

He felt the satisfying resolution of broken frequency finding its match. The marble did not resist so much as *remember* — as if the stone had been waiting, all this time, for someone to tell it that it was allowed to be alive again. The silver light moved through the grey like a hand through water, and where it passed, the cold clinical certainty of the system's answer gave way to the irregular warmth of living limestone, with all the small flaws and seams that life requires.

The geyser shivered. The clinical runes that had been etched into its surface by the system's processing cracked and fell away, their violet-black light fading to a dull, pulsing glow that matched the rhythm of the Aegis-Shield overhead — a wounded throb, like a heartbeat slowing toward sleep. The grey marble reweaved itself into living limestone, its surface pitting and cracking as the natural processes of the earth reclaimed the space that the system had tried to optimize. Moss, that defiant green of things that grow without permission, crept into the new seams; a thread of honest water — the cold trickle of a spring finding its old path — began to well from the base. The Rift was not gone. It was, Lukas had learned, never wholly gone; the wounds of the multiverse scarred rather than vanished. But the bleeding had stopped. The noise had returned to the stone.

"Rift sealed," Nyssa reported, and her golden eyes tracked the resonance-seeker's reading with the particular focus of a woman who had spent her life in the shadows and had learned to find comfort in the measured, scientific rhythm of the mending. "That's the twenty-seventh this month."

Lukas lowered his spear, his silver light dimming to a watchful glow. The geyser was quiet now — no longer a wound but a scar, a reminder of the battle that had been fought and the price that had been paid. The limestone was warm beneath his feet, its surface still carrying the particular vibration of the dimensional frequency that had been sealed within it, a slow, settled hum like a child who had finally stopped crying. He looked at the moss creeping into the seams and felt the quiet rightness of a world that was allowed to be imperfect, to be growing, to be alive.

He felt tired. Not the exhaustion of battle, not the hollow starvation of the Anchor-state, but the quiet weariness of a person who had been carrying the world on his shoulders for a very long time and had finally been allowed to set it down — or, more truly, to set it into the hands of the many, where it had belonged all along. His silver light was dimmer than it had been during the war — not extinguished, but tempered, its frequency reduced to a steady, measured glow that pulsed in rhythm with the heartbeat of the world he had helped to save. The needle, not the pillar. The tool, not the weapon. And in the tempering, he had found a unexpected peace.

Nyssa moved to his side, the seeker's green script reflecting in her golden eyes. She was thinner than she had been at the Harvest, the lean economy of a scout who lived more on the road than off it now; but her posture carried none of the old wary stillness. She stood as a Mender stands — present, grounded, alive. "The Heimfeld delegation wants to name the plaza after you," she said, in her clipped register, the words landing like a report she did not entirely endorse. "Hestian's already drafted the objection. Something about 'precedent and the grain stores.'"

"Tell them no," Lukas said. "Tell them name it for the Menders. Or the moss."

"Hestian will like the moss," Nyssa said, and the crooked lift of her mouth was, for Nyssa, a full sentence and a half.

They stood together at the edge of the sealed geyser, two people who had walked the Open Road and found, at its end, not a destination but a practice — the daily discipline of mending a world that had chosen to remain noisy. The Aegis overhead pulsed its seamless, watchful warmth. The dwarven city moved through the rhythms of reconstruction below them, hammers on metal, the honest clang of a people who had decided that even a Rift could be tended. And for a long moment, neither of them spoke; the comfortable silence of two who had earned the right to share it was its own kind of music.

Suddenly, the violet butterfly on his shoulder began to glow.

The butterfly had been with him since the Maw — a constant, silent companion that sat on his shoulder as he traveled the Seven Realms, its wings opening and closing in the slow, rhythmic dance of a creature that had nowhere to be and all the time in the world to get there. It had been with him through the sealing of the Rifts, through the mending of the wounds, through the long, difficult work of building a peace that was not perfect but was real. It had never spoken. It had never needed to. Its presence was a language — the language of a brother who had chosen to be everywhere and nowhere, to be the silence that protected the noise.

But now the butterfly was glowing.

Its wings caught the light of the setting sun — the warm gold of a true evening, no longer the bruised-violet of the war — and scattered it into patterns that made the air shimmer with warmth. The violet color deepened, its surface throbbed with a frequency that Lukas recognized — the irreplaceable resonance of a bond that had survived the Rending and the Pits and the Forge and the Mission and the Maw and the moment when two brothers had stood at the center of a collapsing system and had chosen, against all probability, to be each other's anchor. The glow was not the cold, clinical radiance of the old order's light. It was the living warmth of a frequency that had been distributed across the multiverse and was now, slowly and deliberately, gathering itself into a single, impossible point.

A voice echoed through the frequency.

"You're getting better at this, Lukas."

Lukas stopped breathing.

The voice was not loud. It was not clear. It was a whisper — a fragment of a frequency that had been compressed and distributed and was now, slowly and painfully, reassembling itself in the space between the butterfly's wings and Lukas's skin. But it was unmistakable. It was the voice of a boy who had run through golden stalks with his hands in the air, who had caught fireflies in the dusk, who had held his brother's hand in the dark and whispered "The moon remembers" with the unshakeable conviction of a child who believed that the words had power. It was Fenris. It had always been Fenris. And Lukas, who had spent a year learning that the brother he loved was not gone but spread, felt the overwhelming rightness of the voice the way a key feels the exact shape of its lock.

"Fenris?" Lukas whispered, and his voice cracked with a grief so deep it felt like drowning and a joy so fierce it felt like flying — the impossible alloy of the two that only the living, and the nearly-lost, had ever learned to forge.

The butterfly lifted from his shoulder — not fleeing, but rising, with the deliberate grace of a creature that had somewhere specific to be. It settled on the tip of his nose, its wings opening and closing in a slow, rhythmic dance that matched the pulse of the Aegis-Shield overhead, a absurd and holy benediction that made Nyssa's golden eyes widen and, for once, say nothing at all.

"I'm here, brother," the voice said, and it carried the particular weight of a truth that had been waiting seventeen years to be spoken — the full, reunited frequency of a boy who had chosen the music over the silence and had spent those seventeen years learning how to play it everywhere at once. "I'm the silence that protects your noise."

Lukas closed his eyes. Behind his eyelids, the memories came — as frequency, the layered song of a life that had been broken and chosen to continue. The wheat fields, the cellar cave, the sound of his mother's song. Fenris's face as the white-violet fire consumed him, his last smile carrying the heaviness of seventeen years of silence. The cage of lightning, the merge, the moment when two frequencies had become one and the system had shattered. The butterfly on his nose, the brother at his shoulder, the music in the air. He had lost everything. And he had gained everything. And the two were the same thing.

He realized then that the Wolfhearts hadn't been divided by the Rift. They had been distributed by it. Fenris was the atmosphere — the invisible, omnipresent frequency that surrounded the world and protected it from the silence, the breathing presence in every violet wing and every silver chord of the Aegis. Lukas was the anchor — the visible, tangible presence that held the world together and gave it form, the stubborn hand on the spear and the stone. And the noise — the beautiful, terrible, uncontrollable noise of being alive — was their bond. The thread that connected them across the void. The frequency that had survived the Rending and the Pits and the Forge and the Mission and the Maw and the moment when the world had ended and had been reborn from the wreckage.

The sky wasn't bruised anymore.

It was a deep, crystalline blue — the color of a morning that had never been touched by the Rending, never been scarred by the violet light of the Rifts. The Aegis-Shield pulsed overhead, its silver-violet web stretching from the Spire's peak to the outer walls of the city, a translucent canopy that filtered the light and held back the worst of the dimensional instability that still lingered in the atmosphere. But the instability was fading. The Rifts were closing. The system was winding down. And the world — noisy, messy, chaotic, beautiful — was learning, slowly and painfully, how to exist without the calculations that had defined it for seventeen thousand cycles.

Lukas opened his eyes and looked up at that blue, and the butterfly lifted from his nose and circled his head once, twice, a joyful spiral, before settling back onto his shoulder as if it had simply been confirming a fact it had always known. Nyssa stood beside him, her golden eyes tracking the blue with the measured calm of a scout who had spent her life reading danger and had finally, after everything, learned to read peace.

"The moon remembers those who endure, Lukas," Nyssa whispered, and her voice carried the particular warmth of a woman who had found her purpose in the shadows and was not yet ready to step into the light — but who had, nonetheless, walked onto a ship and flown to the edge of the world to tend its wounds, and who had, in the tending, become something she had never been in the old order: free.

"And our scars are the map to tomorrow," Lukas replied, and his silver light burned low and steady in his bones, his amber eyes fixed on the butterfly that sat on the tip of his nose — no, had lifted, now rested on his shoulder — with the unshakeable certainty of a man who had learned that the wound and the wisdom were the same thing, that the scar was not a failure of healing but the record of it.

The butterfly pulsed once — a single, warm beat that carried the particular weight of a message that needed no words, that had never needed words, that had been spoken in the wheat fields and the cellar cave and the heart of the Maw and was being spoken still, in every violet wing and every silver chord and every irreplaceable note of the noise.

*I'm here. I'm everywhere. And I'm not done yet.*

The Fall was complete. The empires had dissolved. The system had crashed. The Auditor's calculation had been corrupted by the noise of two brothers who had chosen, against all probability, to be each other's anchor. And the multiverse — noisy, messy, chaotic, beautiful — was no longer a calculation to be solved. It was a symphony to be heard.

Lukas Wolfheart stood at the edge of the Great Geyser, the butterfly on his nose — then his shoulder, then nowhere and everywhere — the spear in his hand, the silver light of his Ascension burning low and steady in the bones of a body that had been pushed beyond its limits and had learned, through the long, painful process of recovery, how to function again. He looked at the sky — at the deep, crystalline blue that stretched from horizon to horizon, at the silver web of the Aegis that flared with a steady, watchful warmth — and he felt something that no equation could contain.

Not hope. Not grief. Not the desperate, defiant fury that had carried him through the Maw and up the Black Glass Tower and into the heart of the Auditor's domain.

He felt *peace*.

The quiet peace of a man who had lost everything and had found, in the wreckage, the only thing that mattered. The peace of a brother who had been separated from his sibling by the void and had found him again, in the frequency of a butterfly's wings. The peace of a hero who had been offered a crown and had refused it, choosing instead the long, difficult road of mending — the road that stretched from the Great Geyser in Heimfeld to the Sunken Isles, from the Shattered Wastes to the Dawn Court, from the edge of the multiverse to the center of the world.

He thought of Alyra, then — of the Queen who had stayed to hold the many, whose violet fire burned steady in the Spire's core, who had told him to come back and had meant it with the fierce tenderness of a woman who had learned that love was the only load worth carrying. He thought of Hestian and his ledgers, of the Council that argued and the market that overlapped and the children who chased the butterflies through the noise. He thought of Zaphrium, distributed into the silver of the Shield, the silence that protected the noise. And he thought of Fenris, everywhere, the music beneath it all, the brother who had chosen to be the atmosphere so that Lukas could be the anchor, so that the noise could continue.

The road was long. The Rifts were many. The system, though winding down, was not yet wholly still; somewhere in the void-space of its processing architecture, the wounded echo of the Auditor's calculation still turned, faint and failing, toward an answer it would never reach. There would be more wounds to tend. There would be more nights on honest earth. There would be, Lukas knew with the settled certainty of a Mender, no final page, only the next note in the symphony.

But the note would be played. The noise would continue. And that, he had learned, was the only victory worth having.

He slept that night in a bed of honest earth — no palace, no Spire, no crystal chamber. Just the ground beneath him, the sky above him, and the irrepressible sound of a world that was alive, that was messy, that was beautiful, that was *noisy*. The butterfly settled at the edge of his sight, a small violet certainty against the deep blue dark, and kept the time.

A Hero who was mending.

An Error who had finally found his home.


The mending had taken the better part of the day, and the dwarven Menders of Heimfeld had watched it with the assessing stillness of a people who measured worth in stone and silence. An old stonemason — her beard gone the honest silver of a long life, her hands mapped with the squared scars of a thousand seams set — had stepped forward when Lukas lowered the spear, and had laid her own rough palm against the new limestone. She had closed her eyes, the wordless concentration of a craftsperson reading a surface by feel, and had nodded once — the gruff benediction of a people who did not praise easily and meant it when they did.

"She sings true," the stonemason said, in the blunt cadence of the forge-tunnels. "Not the old hum. A new one. Lower. Warmer. Like the rock remembers it was alive." She looked at Lukas with eyes that had seen the geyser bleed for fifty years and had, until today, believed it never would stop. "You taught the stone its own name again, Wolfheart. That is a thing we could not do. That is a thing we will remember."

Lukas had not known what to say to that. He had offered the awkward incline of a man unaccustomed to being thanked for the only work left to him, and the stonemason had snorted — the affectionate dismissal of a elder who had decided he was acceptable despite his strangeness — and had turned back to her buckets and her bindings, already planning the slow revegetation of the plaza's edges. The Menders, Lukas had learned, did not linger on victory. They moved to the next wound. That, he thought, was the hard wisdom of the new order: not to celebrate the healed scar, but to find the next one before it bled.

The crowd that had gathered at the plaza's edge — dwarven children released from the lower schools, a Selkhar delegate who had come to observe the sealing, a handful of human merchants whose wagons had paused on the Heimfeld road — had broken into a uncoordinated cheer when the runes fell, and then, with the unselfconscious ease of people who had survived the finalization, had simply gone back to their business. No parade. No decree. The geyser was a scar now, not a spectacle; the city had work to do. Lukas found, in that ordinary dismissal, a deeper honor than any monument Hestian would object to: the noise of the world, resuming.

It was in the quiet after the crowd thinned that the butterfly began to glow. And in the suspended moment before Fenris's voice arrived, Lukas felt the whole impossible architecture of the last seventeen years settle into a single point — the wheat and the fire, the cage and the merge, the Anchor-state and the Open Road, the brother distributed and the brother anchored, all of it gathered into the small, violet glow at his shoulder. He had spent so long bracing for the loss that he had not permitted himself to imagine the staggering alternative: that Fenris had not been taken, but given; that the music had not been silenced, but sung everywhere at once; that the boy who had whispered "the moon remembers" in a cellar cave had, against every calculation the Auditor had made, been right.

When the voice came — "You're getting better at this, Lukas" — it carried not only the irreplaceable resonance of his brother but the unmistakable texture of Fenris's old teasing, the fond exasperation of a younger sibling who had always known Lukas would take the hardest road and had, in the end, taken the harder one himself. Lukas's breath caught and held. The geyser's new limestone hummed beneath his boots. The Aegis pulsed its seamless, watchful blue. And for one impossible, particular heartbeat, the Wolfheart brothers were not distributed across a multiverse but together — here, at the edge of a sealed Rift, in the deep crystalline evening of a world that had chosen to stay.

The realization that followed — that they had not been divided by the Rift but distributed by it — arrived as a bodily knowing, the way the shoulder knows the crush of a spear it has carried for years. Fenris was the atmosphere: the invisible, omnipresent frequency that surrounded the world and protected it from the silence, present in every violet wing and every silver chord of the Aegis, breathing in the spaces the old order had tried to empty. Lukas was the anchor: the visible, tangible presence that held the world together and gave it form, the stubborn hand on the spear and the stone. And the noise — that beautiful, terrible, uncontrollable noise of being alive — was the bond between them, the thread that crossed the void, the frequency that had survived the Rending and the Pits and the Forge and the Mission and the Maw and the moment when the world had ended and been reborn from the wreckage. They were not two halves of a broken thing. They were two aspects of a single, distributed whole, and the distribution was not a wound but a design — the resilient shape of a bond the Auditor's system had never been able to solve because it had never been able to comprehend that love might choose to be everywhere at once.

Nyssa, who had said nothing through the whole of it, let out a slow breath — the wordless exhale of a scout who had seen the impossible and had recorded it under *accepted*, because the impossible was, after all, the only thing the last year had reliably produced. She holstered the seeker with the practiced motion of a woman who did not need to measure what she had just witnessed; some things, her stillness said, were beyond the instrument. Lukas glanced at her and found her golden eyes on him, and in them the unspoken acknowledgment of two people who had walked the Open Road together and had, against all probability, arrived.

"We should report to the Spire," Nyssa said, when the butterfly had settled and the blue had deepened toward true evening. Her voice was the clipped register of a Mender transitioning from wonder back to work — but there was a softened edge to it, the trace of a woman who had just watched a dead brother speak and had decided not to analyze the miracle. "Hestian will want the count. Twenty-seven sealed. He'll put it in a ledger and argue with it."

"Let him argue," Lukas said, and the quiet warmth in his voice was the warmth of a man who had made his peace with the ledgers of the world. "The count is the noise, Nyssa. Every sealed Rift is a note. He can tally them all he likes. The symphony plays whether he balances the columns or not."

She considered that, the feline tilt of her head that meant she was storing the thought for later use. "He'll say that's not how ledgers work."

"Then he'll be wrong, and the moon will remember that too."

They laughed, the easy sound of two people who had earned the right to it — not the first genuine laugh of a man relieved of a burden, as on the docking field, but the settled laugh of a Mender who had done the day's work and trusted the tomorrow to bring another. The butterfly pulsed once on Lukas's shoulder, a warm beat of agreement, and the Great Geyser's new limestone hummed its low, true song beneath their boots, and the deep crystalline blue of the unbruised sky held them all — Wolfheart and scout and brother and stone — in the watchful warmth of a world that had chosen, against every calculation, to remain.

In the days that followed, as the *Messenger* carried them from Heimfeld toward the next weeping border and the next scar to tend, Lukas found the quiet rhythm of the mending life settling into him the way the silver-static had once settled into his boots — not as a weight now, but as a belonging. He woke on honest earth more often than not, beneath a sky that was, with each sealed Rift, a particular degree bluer; he ate the travel-bread Nyssa rationed with the unsentimental precision of a scout, and drank the Selkhar-brine, and listened to the resonance-seeker's green song as it mapped the wounds still waiting ahead. The butterfly rode his shoulder or his nose or the cabin's dim corner, present and absent by turns, the constant reminder that the silence protecting the noise was, itself, his brother. And Alyra's voice — relayed through the communication-crystal in Hestian's measured cadence, with the fierce addition the Queen always appended — came to them at each realm's edge, a warm tether to the center he had left and would, he had promised, return to.

"The Councils hold," Hestian reported on the third day out, his level voice carrying the buried warmth of a man who had learned, against his ledger's objections, to trust the messy thing. "The grain stores hold. The market overlaps. The children chase the butterflies. The Queen says — " a rare hesitation, the sound of Hestian declining to editorialize — "the Queen says the noise is not the same without you, Wolfheart. She did not say it loudly. I am instructed to convey it regardless."

Lukas smiled at the crystal, and the butterfly on his shoulder opened its wings once, a violet benediction, as if Fenris too had heard the Queen's fierce tenderness and had approved. "Tell her the road is long," he said, "but the music plays. Tell her I'm coming home."

And the *Messenger* carried him onward — toward the next Rift, the next scar, the next irreplaceable note in the symphony of a multiverse that had chosen, against every probability the old order had computed, to remain noisy, to remain messy, to remain alive.
The Moon Remembers. And the music never stops.

---

## Epilogue: The Last Letter

*Found in the Spire's archive three mornings after the Final Wave, folded into a hollow of the Heart of the Aegis where the silver-web was thinnest, written in the cramped, elegant hand of a dragon who had spent four centuries learning to keep his claws light on paper. It was addressed to no one, and to everyone.*

---

To the Wolfhearts — to Lukas, who holds the line; to Alyra, who holds the many; to Nyssa, who holds the shadows; and to Fenris, who has become the holding itself —

If you are reading this, then I have finally done what I have been pretending not to do for four hundred years: I have stopped watching.

You will forgive me the deception. It was a long con, and I was very good at it. I told you the Shield was a frequency I could sustain, a membrane of compressed song stretched between the sky and the stone, and for a time that was true. But a shield is only as strong as the thing it is willing to become, and the thing I was willing to become, in the end, was smaller than a shield and larger than a sky. I am the silence now. The quiet space between the notes where the world breathes. I would say I am sorry for the sadness of that, but I have learned, watching you, that silence is only sad when it is empty — and this silence is full. It is full of you.

I want you to know how it looked from where I sat. Four hundred years is a long time to watch a world almost fail, and mine was the particular vantage of someone who had seen every ending before and was always bracing for the next one. I watched the Spire rise and the Spire fall and the Spire rise again. I watched kings mistake power for safety and safety for worth. I watched the Auditor — that great, patient, hungry arithmetic — try to subtract the noise from the world, and I believed, in the grey centuries, that it would win, because arithmetic is patient and love is not.

I was wrong. I was wrong in the way that matters most: gently, completely, and for the best of reasons.

You were the thing I could not calculate. Lukas — the boy who held a dying shield with nothing but the force of his own refusing, who chose to be broken rather than let the world be tidy. Alyra — the girl who learned to strike a frequency instead of a throat, who governed a messy thing by listening to its noise instead of silencing it. Nyssa — who moved through shadows so long that the light had to come looking for her. And Fenris — the one I mourn the most, the one who took the hardest road, who chose to be scattered so that he could be everywhere, who is now the hush that protects the roar. I watched each of you, and I understood, at last, what the Auditor never could: that a perfect thing is a dead thing, and that the world is worth protecting not in spite of its mess but because of it.

So I have left you this. Not gold. Not a throne. Not a weapon that can be swung. Just a letter, and a silence, and the thing you already knew: that I loved you. That I loved you the way an old thing loves the young — with envy, and with pride, and with the particular ache of someone who knows he will not see where you go. I will not see the Council that learns to argue without blood. I will not see the markets overlap, or the children chase the butterflies, or the Rifts slowly close, or the moon rise over a world that has finally learned to be loud. But I have seen enough. I have seen the best thing I have ever witnessed.

Guard the noise. It is the only thing the silence cannot eat. When the night is dark and the wind is cold and the world seems to have forgotten the sound of laughter, remember that the moon remembers those who endure — and that your scars are the map to tomorrow.

Do not grieve for long. Grieve hard, and then come back to the work. The work is the thing. The mending. The arguing. The daily, stubborn, glorious noise of being alive. That is what I leave you, and that is everything.

When you hear the wind and it sounds like an old dragon clearing his throat — that is me, watching. I never stopped watching. I just learned to do it from inside the sky.

The moon remembers, children. And so do I.

— Zaphrium Drakon

---

**Book 4: Fall of Empires — END.**
